y , a n d U t i l i t i e s 1 1 5 6 CHAPTER I INTRODUCTION The rapid advancement of geospatial and data analytics technology has transformed decision-making across many sectors, including disaster risk reduction and emergency management. However , disaster risk remains a serious concern in the Philippines, which ranks first in disaster risk among 193 countries, with a W orldRiskIndex score of 46.56 in 2025 (Bündnis Entwicklung Hilft & Ruhr University Bochum, 2025). T imely , location-based information is critical in an emergency , and W eb-GIS platforms are increasingly used for decision support, real-time monitoring, early warning, and coordination among stakeholders during hazard events (Daud et al., 2024). Under Republic Act No. 10121, the NDRRMC is the body that sets policy , coordinates, supervises, monitors, and evaluates disaster risk reduction and management in the country (Republic Act No. 10121, 2010). Even with this law , many municipalities still depend on phone calls, text messages, and two-way radios, which leads to delayed reporting and no real-time location tracking (Consumo et al., 2026). The absence of a centralized geospatial reporting system also makes it dif ficult for local of fices to see where incidents are concentrated and where response is slowest. Studies indicate that geospatial analysis can help by showing incident hotspots and areas with slow response, which supports better use of staf f and response teams (Aporto et al., 2026). 7 The Negros Island Region was created in 2024 and groups Negros Occidental, Negros Oriental, and Siquijor , together with their cities, municipalities, and barangays, into one region (Republic Act No. 12000, 2024). Before this law , Negros Occidental was under Region 6 while Negros Oriental and Siquijor were under Region 7 (Manila Bulletin, 2024). Within this newly formed region, the T alisay City Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Of fice (DRRMO) and the Bureau of Fire Protection (BFP) Station do not have an existing system for real-time incident recording and coordination, hindering trend analysis and timely response. The proposed Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration (GAOIRS) integrates geospatial incident mapping, kernel density hotspot detection, and Prophet-based forecasting to help DRRMO and BFP personnel respond to NDRRMC-catered incidents in the region more ef ficiently . In T alisay City , the DRRMO and BFP Station still relied on manual processes such as phone calls, radio, and separate logbooks, which delays reporting, allows errors in location details, and limits the real-time view of ongoing incidents. Geospatial analysis can ef fectively map incident distribution and highlight high-risk zones, underscoring the need for a digital, map-based response system that supports faster and more accurate decisions (Han & Liu, 2025). Researchers in nearby Oton, Iloilo similarly recommend digitizing and geolocating incident records and using real-time GIS dashboards for more ef ficient and fair emergency response planning (Aporto et al., 2026). 8 The purpose of this study was to develop the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration for reporting, verifying, dispatching, and monitoring NDRRMC-catered incidents in the Negros Island Region. The study aimed to improve response ef ficiency by automating incident intake, verification, and nearest-responder dispatch, which cuts down the time needed to report, confirm, and act on an incident. This helps DRRMO and BFP personnel quickly identify high-risk areas and respond faster , strengthening disaster risk reduction and public safety in the region. 1.1 Background of the Study The City Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Of fice (CDRRMO) and the Bureau of Fire Protection (BFP) Station serve as the primary agencies responsible for coordinating disaster preparedness, emergency response, rescue operations, and recovery ef forts within the city (Republic Act No. 10121, 2010). As disasters such as floods, landslides, fires, and road accidents continue to af fect communities, and with the city's growing population, both of fices play a vital role in receiving reports, verifying incidents, dispatching response units, and monitoring cases across their jurisdiction. The of fices are staf fed by trained personnel who are tasked with recording incident reports, coordinating with response units, and relaying information to city and regional authorities. Currently , the DRRMO and BFP Station rely on fragmented communication channels, including phone calls, text messages, and two-way radios, for incident reporting (Lagmay et al., 2021). When a resident calls or 9 radios in a report, personnel manually note the caller's description of the incident type and location in a logbook or dispatch sheet, and each of fice keeps its own records separately . This process requires responders to manually verify locations and consolidate reports, resulting in delays, incomplete records, and dif ficulties in monitoring emergency situations (UNDRR, 2023). Another challenge is the lack of a centralized geospatial system for visualizing incident locations and identifying the appropriate response jurisdiction (Esri, 2024). Without spatial analytics, DRRMO and BFP personnel have limited capability to identify high-risk areas, prioritize incidents, and support evidence-based planning for resource allocation (Lagmay et al., 2021). In response to these challenges, the researchers proposed the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration to address the DRRMO and BFP Station's challenges in managing NDRRMC-catered incidents. The system was designed to let citizens submit geotagged incident reports, verify reports before dispatch, identify and dispatch the nearest available response unit, and generate geospatial hotspot maps and Prophet-based trend forecasts to identify high-risk areas. Through this solution, DRRMO and BFP personnel will be able to access and analyze incident data more ef ficiently , enabling faster response, more accurate reporting, and more strategic resource allocation across the Negros Island Region. 1 0 1.2 Objectives of the Study The general objective of this study was to design and develop the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration that enables ef ficient reporting, coordinated response, and monitoring of NDRRMC-catered incidents in the Negros Island Region. Specifically , this study aimed to: 1. Design and develop the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration with the following features: 1.1. incident report recording and management; 1.2. incident response; 1.3. geospatial mapping interface with identification of the city and congressional district of incidents; 1.4. monitoring and analysis; and 1.5. report generation. 2. T est the functionality of the developed system; 3. Evaluate the quality of the system using the ISO/IEC 25010:201 1 criteria and ISO 9241-1 1:2018 measures. 4. Develop a User ’ s Guide for the developed system. 1.3 Significance of the Study This study is significant as it develops a Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration that enhances the 1 1 ability of local government units to report, monitor , and respond to disaster incidents through a centralized mapping platform. By integrating geotagged reporting, geofencing, spatial analytics, and real-time status tracking, the system improves the accuracy , accessibility , and timeliness of disaster information. Its development contributes to more ef ficient emergency response, better identification of high-risk areas, and informed disaster risk reduction planning at the local government level. The results of this study will be significant to the following: City Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Office (CDRRMO). The developed system will help disaster personnel record, verify , and monitor incident reports more ef ficiently through a centralized platform. It will improve coordination among response units, reduce delays in emergency response, and support better decision-making through organized and location-based incident information. Local Government Units (LGUs). The system will provide LGUs with reliable geospatial data that can support disaster preparedness, resource allocation, and emergency planning. It will enable local of ficials to identify high-risk areas and prioritize communities that require immediate intervention during disaster situations. Citizens and Communities. The study will benefit citizens by providing a convenient platform for submitting geotagged disaster reports and tracking the status of their reported incidents. It will strengthen communication between the 1 2 community and emergency responders while promoting faster reporting and improved public safety . Future Researchers. This study will serve as a valuable reference for future researchers interested in disaster management, geographic information systems, and data analytics. It may provide foundational knowledge for developing more advanced disaster response and decision-support systems. 1.4 Scope and Delimitation of the Study This study focuses on the design, development, testing, and evaluation of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration (GAOIRS) for incidents catered by the NDRRMC within the Negros Island Region (NIR). The system has three user roles: Citizen, who submits incident reports; Response Unit, consisting of BFP and DRRMO personnel who respond to verified incidents; and Administrator , who manages the system, verifies and approves incident reports, and user permissions. Citizen-submitted reports undergo verification before being dispatched to an appropriate response unit. Key features of the system include incident reporting and management, response dispatch, geospatial mapping, geofencing, incident monitoring, Kernel Density Estimation (KDE), and report generation. The system is developed using React, V ite, and T ailwind CSS for the frontend; Node.js and Express for the backend; PostgreSQL for data storage; Leaflet.js for geospatial mapping; Chart.js for data visualization; and Python for KDE analysis. The 1 3 system is accessible through a modern web browser and requires a stable internet connection. While the system provides features for incident reporting, response coordination, monitoring, and spatial analysis, it has several limitations. The system is limited to incidents catered by the NDRRMC within the NIR and does not support coordination or incident sharing between dif ferent local government units or jurisdictions. It is intended only for the DRRMO and BFP as response agencies and does not include the police or other government agencies as system users. The system does not predict natural hazards or issue hazard warnings, as these functions remain outside the scope of the study . The KDE feature is limited to identifying spatial concentrations or hotspots of reported incidents and is not intended to predict individual incidents or future disasters. In addition, real-time reporting, mapping, dispatch, and status monitoring require internet connectivity , and system performance may be af fected by poor or unstable network connections. 1.5 Definition of T erms The following key terms have been operationally defined based on their specific usage within the context of this study . These definitions aim to provide clarity and ensure a consistent understanding of the concepts central to the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration (GAOIRS), particularly those related to incident reporting, emergency 1 4 response, geospatial mapping, spatial analysis, and disaster incident management. Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration (GAOIRS). In this study , this term refers to the web-based system developed to record, manage, map, dispatch, monitor , and analyze disaster incidents reported within the Negros Island Region. Incident Report. In this study , this term refers to the information submitted by a citizen regarding a disaster or emergency incident, including its location, description, and photo evidence. Citizen. In this study , this term refers to a registered system user who submits incident reports and monitors the status of their submitted reports. Response Unit. In this study , this term refers to personnel from the T alisay City Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Of fice and Bureau of Fire Protection who are responsible for responding to verified incidents assigned to them through the system. Administrator . In this study , this term refers to an authorized user responsible for managing system operations, accounts, permissions, and other administrative functions within GAOIRS. Incident V erification. In this study , this term refers to the process of reviewing and validating a citizen-submitted incident report before it is dispatched to an appropriate response unit. 1 5 Incident Response. In this study , this term refers to the actions taken by designated response units after a reported incident has been verified and assigned for response. Geographic Information System (GIS). In this study , this term refers to the mapping component of GAOIRS used to display and manage incident locations and other location-based information. Geofencing. In this study , this term refers to the use of predefined geographic boundaries in the system to identify the barangay , city , and congressional district where a reported incident is located. Incident Mapping. In this study , this term refers to the visualization of reported incidents on an interactive map using location data and color-coded markers. Kernel Density Estimation (KDE). In this study , this term refers to the spatial analysis method used to identify areas with higher concentrations of reported incidents and visualize incident hotspots. Incident Monitoring. In this study , this term refers to the process of viewing and tracking reported incidents and their response status through the system. Response Dispatch. In this study , this term refers to the assignment of verified incidents to an appropriate response unit based on the system's incident and location information. 1 6 Incident Status T racking. In this study , this term refers to the monitoring of the progress of a reported incident from verification and response through its subsequent status updates. Incident Report Generation. In this study , this term refers to the system's capability to compile and export recorded incident information into reports for monitoring and documentation purposes. 1 7 CHAPTER II REVIEW OF RELA TED LITERA TURE AND SYSTEMS This chapter presents a brief review of related literature, studies, and the interrelated system as the basis of making and developing the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration. 2.1 Foreign Literature Comprehensive Analysis of the Use of W eb-GIS for Natural Hazard Management: A Systematic Review This study reviewed how W eb-GIS is used in natural hazard management. Following the PRISMA process, the researchers screened 1,775 articles published from 2014 to 2023 and analyzed 65 of them. The studies were grouped into eight subtopics, including decision support systems, real-time monitoring and early warning, visualization and user interface design, and multi-stakeholder collaboration. The review also noted that W eb-GIS studies were fewer in low- and middle-income countries. These findings support the relevance of GAOIRS, which uses a map-based dashboard, real-time monitoring, and shared access for dif ferent user roles to manage incidents (Daud et al., 2024). A Near-Real-T ime Global Landslide Incident Reporting T ool Demonstrator Using Social Media and Artificial Intelligence This study developed a near-real-time tool for reporting landslide incidents. The system continuously monitored social media, collected photographs, and used a trained image classification model to keep only the posts likely to show landslides. In a real-world deployment, it detected landslide 1 8 reports with a precision of 76%. The study showed that incident reports can be captured and filtered quickly instead of waiting for manual reporting. These findings support the relevance of GAOIRS, which relies on fast incident intake with photo evidence and includes a verification step before responders are dispatched, since incoming reports may be inaccurate (Pennington et al., 2022). Designing Multimodal Interactive Dashboard of Disaster Management Systems This study designed interactive dashboards that present text, image, audio, and video information about disasters in one place. The researchers first studied social media data to identify the requirements, then built the dashboard designs, and had experts evaluate them using heuristics. The evaluators gave positive feedback on the designs. The study highlighted the value of bringing dif ferent kinds of incident information into a single screen and of evaluating dashboard usability with experts. This relates to GAOIRS, whose monitoring dashboard also shows incident details, photo evidence, and status updates in one view (AlAbdulaali et al., 2022). A Study on GIS-Based Spatial Analysis of Emergency Response for Disaster Management: Focusing on Seoul This study analyzed the operation of fire emergency dispatch in Seoul, examined the impact of the “golden time” on dispatch, and identified factors and improvement measures for ef fective dispatch within prime time. The results showed that rescue dispatch activities dif fered depending on the nature of the incident. The number of calls was highest in summer (15,652), compared with spring (13,189), fall (13,128), and winter (14,545), and Saturday was the day of 1 9 the week with the most calls. The spatial analysis also showed that rescue incidents were concentrated in some areas. These findings support the hotspot map and time-based reports in GAOIRS, which help show where and when incidents cluster so that response units can be prepared (Park et al., 2024). Population Access to Hospital Emergency Departments: The Spatial Analysis in Public Health Research This study examined how spatial analysis can be used to evaluate access to hospital emergency departments (EDs) in Poland. The researchers compared the distribution and travel-time accessibility of EDs in 201 1 and 2021 using GIS-based distance measurement across communes. The findings showed that reorganizing the ED network nationally improved overall spatial accessibility , but some areas, including cities that lost nearby facilities, saw accessibility decline. This reinforces that comparing spatial data across two points in time is a useful way to evaluate whether a service network is reaching the people who need it. This supports the practice in GAOIRS of using geospatial mapping and hotspot analysis to evaluate whether incident response coverage is reaching the areas that need it most (Kisiała et al., 2022). GIS Based Approach of Surface T ransport Network Analysis This study used GIS-based network analysis to determine the best route for healthcare responders to reach an incident location in the Katol metropolitan area. The researchers compared an enhanced GIS route against the standard shortest-path route and found that the optimized route improved travel time by 22%. The study demonstrated that in a densely populated area, an enhanced GIS-based routing approach outperforms a simple shortest-path method when 2 0 arriving quickly at an incident matters most. This supports the value of GIS network analysis in GAOIRS for helping response units identify the fastest way to reach a verified incident, not just the shortest path (Gupta & Khode, 2025). The Role of GIS in Disaster Response: Improving Efficiency and Overcoming Challenges This review examined how GIS supports disaster response, drawing on studies covering risk assessment, real-time monitoring, evacuation planning, and post-disaster recovery . The researchers found that GIS's integration with technologies such as remote sensing, AI, and machine learning has expanded its ability to support faster and more accurate decisions, but that its use in disaster management still faces challenges related to data quality , system interoperability , and user capabilities. The review highlighted the need for stronger integration between GIS and other disaster management systems. This supports designing GAOIRS around simple role-based access, data verification, and testing with actual DRRMO and BFP users, rather than around its mapping features alone (Han & Liu, 2025). Emergency Incident Detection from Crowdsourced W aze Data Using Bayesian Information Fusion This study addressed the delay caused by the traditional reactive approach to emergency reporting, in which a person must call an emergency number , by using crowd-generated reports from the W aze platform to detect incidents. Because the reliability of report sources and the uncertainty about the time and place of reports are major challenges, the researchers built a Bayesian framework that models this uncertainty and integrates reports across space and 2 1 time. When tested with W aze data and of ficial incident records from Nashville, T ennessee, the method outperformed strong baselines in F1-score and AUC. This supports the need in GAOIRS to check the reliability of citizen reports and to tag reports that refer to the same incident (Senarath et al., 2020). Spatiotemporal Clustering and Analysis of Road Accident Hotspots by Exploiting GIS T echnology and Kernel Density Estimation This study applied kernel density estimation (KDE) together with spatial autocorrelation methods to identify road accident hotspots using real crash data from the United Kingdom. The researchers used GIS tools to detect clusters of accidents and to examine how parameter choices, such as bandwidth, af fect which areas appear as hotspots. The findings showed that GIS-based KDE, when properly tuned, produces reliable and interpretable hotspot maps that can guide safety interventions. This supports the use of KDE for the heatmap feature in GAOIRS and shows why the bandwidth setting must be chosen carefully to produce a reliable hotspot map (Kazmi et al., 2022). Forecasting the Human Cost of Disasters under Sustainable Development Goal: A T ime Series Analysis Using Facebook Prophet Model This study used the Facebook Prophet model to forecast disaster-related deaths from 2024 to 2030, using historical data from 2001 to 2023 across 168 countries. The researchers calculated annual mortality rates for each country and used Prophet to project future rates, finding an average global mortality rate of 13.38 deaths per million people with significant variation across countries. The study showed that the Prophet can turn historical disaster records into forward-looking estimates that support prioritization and planning. This is the 2 2 basis of the Prophet-based incident forecasting used in GAOIRS to project incident volume from historical DRRMO and BFP records (Ahmad et al., 2025). 2.2 Local Literature Geospatial Analysis of Emergency Incidents and Disaster Response Management in Oton, Iloilo This study examined emergency response operations in Oton, Iloilo, to find gaps in service coverage and response ef ficiency . The researchers digitized 1,428 Patient Care Report forms from July to December 2024 and analyzed incident counts and average response times by barangay using Getis-Ord hotspot analysis and Moran's I. Urbanized barangays had more incidents and faster responses, while remote and rural barangays had fewer incidents and longer responses, and response times were longest in the evening and on weekends. The study recommended digitizing and geolocating incident records and using real-time GIS dashboards. These findings support the heatmap, hotspot, and dashboard features of GAOIRS (Aporto et al., 2026). A Spatiotemporal Analysis of Fire Incidents in Pampanga Province of Philippines: Inputs for Fire Prevention Programs This study examined fire incident data of the Bureau of Fire Protection in Pampanga from 2013 to 2020. After cleaning the data, the researchers mapped and charted the incidents to study patterns in space and time. Fires were most likely on Sundays and Mondays in the highly urbanized cities of Angeles, Mabalacat, and San Fernando, mostly in March and December and between 2:00 and 3:00 PM. The study also found that barangays in flood-prone areas were likely to experience fire cases, and it recommended the spatiotemporal 2 3 method for planning by the fire bureau. This supports the trend analysis and time-based reports in GAOIRS (de Leon & Miranda, 2022). Datasets of Fire and Crime Incidents in Pampanga, Philippines This study collected and analyzed fire and crime incident datasets from the Bureau of Fire Protection and the Philippine National Police for the province of Pampanga. The researchers cleaned, normalized, and mapped both datasets to identify temporal patterns, finding that fires mostly occurred during the dry season and that crime was particularly high in December . The study showed that agency-held incident records, once digitized and cleaned, can reveal clear temporal patterns useful for planning. This supports the practice in GAOIRS of digitizing and cleaning DRRMO and BFP incident records to reveal similar patterns for planning response deployment (Miranda et al., 2023). Development of Crime Reporting System to Identify Patterns of Crime in Laguna This study developed a crime reporting system to identify patterns of crime in the province of Laguna, Philippines. The researchers digitized crime records and applied pattern analysis to identify recurring characteristics of reported incidents by location and time. The system demonstrated that a digital incident reporting and pattern-analysis tool can help local authorities move from manually reviewing paper records to systematically identifying trends. This supports the design of GAOIRS as a system that digitizes incident reports and analyzes them for recurring patterns to guide response planning (V illarica et al., 2022). 2 4 Black Spot Cluster Analysis of Road Crash Involving Public Utility V ehicles (PUV) Along Commonwealth A venue Using Kernel Density Estimation This study identified road crash hotspots involving public utility vehicles along Commonwealth A venue in Quezon City , which is known as the “killer highway” of Metro Manila, with an average of 17 fatal road crashes per year from 2017 to 2021. The researchers used the crash records of the Metropolitan Manila Development Authority and applied kernel density estimation using free open-source software. The study revealed five fatal crash locations, with high-density crashes from 7:00 to 10:00 AM and from 5:00 to 8:00 PM, specifically on Fridays at Regalado A venue. The authors said the results can be used to identify black spots and to develop targeted interventions. This supports the use of KDE on agency incident records, as done in GAOIRS (Ballarta et al., 2024). V isualization and Geo-Mapping of Philippine Fire Incidents This study explored how data visualization and analysis can extract useful information for planning and decision-making, given the rapid increase in fire incidents recorded in the Philippines from 2013 to 2016. The researchers used the 2012 to 2016 Philippine Nationwide Fire Incident Statistics data of the Bureau of Fire Protection, which was provided through Open Data Philippines. After cleaning and reprocessing the data, they developed a dynamic system called FireStatPH using Flask. This shows that recorded incident data can be turned into visual and map-based outputs for decision-making, which is the same purpose of the analytics and dashboard features of GAOIRS (Oñate, 2022). 2 5 Building Model for Crime Pattern Analysis Through Machine Learning Using Predictive Analytics This study examined how well decision trees and ensemble trees can classify crime through model development. The researchers ran experiments to improve the ensembles in both classification and regression, and they used the synthetic minority oversampling technique (SMOTE) to address the problem of imbalanced data. Using dif ferent metrics to evaluate each model, they found that the gradient boosted tree had better classification capability than the decision tree and the random forest in both classification and regression. This supports the practice in GAOIRS of comparing several models using evaluation metrics before choosing the one used for incident forecasting (Asor et al., 2022). Compliance with the Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Act of 2010 of Local Government Units in the Province of Negros Occidental, Philippines This study assessed how well the local government units of Negros Occidental comply with the Philippine Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Act, in the areas of risk management practices and institutional arrangements and capacities across the four thematic areas of disaster . The researchers investigated the challenges encountered by the DRRM of ficers and planning coordinators of 32 LGUs. Using descriptive analysis, they found a high level of compliance in prevention and mitigation, preparedness, and response, and a moderate level in rehabilitation and recovery . LGUs with nine or more hazards were rated high, while 4th class LGUs were rated moderate. The study concluded that failure to provide personnel and financial resources to the DRRM of fice endangers the safety of the population. This supports the need for practical 2 6 tools such as GAOIRS that help DRRM of fices in the province manage incidents with the resources they have (Y usay & Caelian, 2022). An Assessment of the Effectiveness of Government's Risk Management on Disaster Prevention, Mitigation, Response, Recovery , and Rehabilitation in Metro Manila, Philippines This study assessed the ef fectiveness of local government units in Metro Manila across four thematic areas of disaster risk reduction and management: preparedness, prevention and mitigation, response, and recovery and rehabilitation. Using a descriptive-evaluative design, the researchers found that all four areas were rated ef fective overall, with disaster preparedness rated highest. The study noted that consistent implementation across LGUs still depends on adequate personnel and resources at the local level. This supports the need for practical tools such as GAOIRS that help DRRMO and BFP of fices in the region carry out NDRRMC-catered response duties with the resources they have (Lagrada & Santiago, 2025). Acceptance of Mobile Application on Disaster Preparedness: T owards Decision Intelligence in Disaster Management This study examined user acceptance of a mobile application for disaster preparedness as a step toward decision intelligence in disaster management. The study is relevant because it shows that acceptance by users is an important measure for disaster-related applications, which is why GAOIRS was evaluated for usability with actual end users (Escolano et al., 2023). 2 7 2.3 Foreign Systems Multi-Hazard W ebGIS Platform for Coastal Regions (MOSAIC) The MOSAIC W ebGIS platform was developed for coastal regions. It gives access to in-situ and remote sensing data and model predictions in one place, and it includes a database of past hazardous events for the western coast of Portugal, organized by risk indicators. The authors said the platform supports both emergency response and long-term risk planning. This supports the use of a historical incident database together with live monitoring in GAOIRS (Rocha et al., 2021). T owards a Digital T win for Supporting Multi-Agency Incident Management in a Smart City This study identified the challenges faced by emergency services in a multi-agency response, such as scattered data and poor real-time information. Using a systems engineering approach, the researchers built a prototype on Microsoft Azure cloud services that lets stakeholders view available resources, send automatic updates, and add real-time weather and traf fic data. The prototype shows how one shared view of an incident can support agencies working together . This supports the shared incident view and the automatic status updates in GAOIRS (W olf et al., 2022). The ERMES Chatbot: A Conversational Communication T ool for Improved Emergency Management and Disaster Risk Reduction The ERMES chatbot is a T elegram-based tool for two-way communication among control centers, field forces, and citizens. The researchers designed, implemented, and validated it using a user-centered approach, and they 2 8 co-designed its functions with emergency management practitioners. The chatbot connects to a web dashboard that shows the collected data and supports mission management and early warning. This supports the design of citizen reporting, response unit updates, and a monitoring dashboard built around user needs in GAOIRS (Urbanelli et al., 2024). Secure Cross-Layer Mobile Sensing Framework for Real-T ime Disaster Reporting and V isualisation Using a Mobile Application This study proposed a secure, scalable mobile system called DEAPP for real-time disaster reporting and visualization. The system connects a native mobile app, a web-based backend, and a cloud database through a cross-layer architecture, and it uses role-based access control, HTTPS encryption, and verified event publication to keep citizen-submitted reports secure and reliable. The researchers also used caching to speed up data access during emergencies. This shows that a citizen-facing mobile reporting app can be built with verification and access control from the start — the same approach GAOIRS takes with its report verification step before dispatch (Mustafa et al., 2025). Sahana Eden Disaster Management Platform Sahana Eden is a free and open-source family of web-based applications that addresses common coordination problems during a disaster , such as finding missing people, managing aid and volunteers, and tracking camps, among government groups, civil society , and the victims themselves. Its core features include mapping for situation awareness and geospatial analysis, requests management, volunteer management, and shelter and organization registries. The project began with volunteers in Sri Lanka after the 2004 tsunami and has 2 9 been used by dozens of governments and non-governmental organizations. It can also provide a management and ticketing interface for crowdsourced data such as that collected in Ushahidi. This supports the combination of mapping and coordination tools in one platform, as in GAOIRS (T readgold, 2022). 2.4 Local Systems iAlerto: A W eb and Mobile Alert System for Pasig City DRRMO with Mobile GPS Service Integration iAlerto was developed to improve incident and emergency reporting by Pasig City residents and to support the Pasig City DRRMO. The developers used Scrum. The mobile application was built with Android Studio, and the web application with CodeIgniter , PHP , and MySQL. The web application manages user profiles, evacuation centers, and incident reports, sends alerts and announcements, and generates reports, while the mobile application supplies the GPS location. T wenty IT professionals and twenty residents and DRRMO employees evaluated it using ISO 9126 functionality criteria, and it was found functionally acceptable. This closely matches the reporting, alert, GPS, and report generation features of GAOIRS (Goh et al., 2023). Application of Spatiotemporal Analysis and Knowledge Discovery for Databases in the Bureau of Fire Protection as Incident Report System: T ool for Improving Fire Services This study developed a fire report management system for the Bureau of Fire Protection that analyzes the space and time characteristics of fires and applies Knowledge Discovery in Databases (KDD) methods to identify patterns of fire incidents in Manila. The developers applied SMOTE and one-hot encoding 3 0 for data processing and used the Agile method as the software development model. This supports the combination of an incident reporting system and pattern analysis in GAOIRS (Balahadia et al., 2020). GPS-Based Emergency Response System for Disaster Risk Reduction and Management This system was developed for the Municipal Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Of fice of Lambunao, Iloilo, to address delayed emergency reporting, the absence of real-time location tracking, and poor coordination between residents and responders. It has a mobile application for residents and responders and a web-based dashboard for the of fice. Residents submit emergency requests with details, images, and an automatically captured GPS location, while responders receive alerts, navigate to the incident, and update their status. The dashboard shows incidents, responder locations, and response progress. It was built with Flutter , PHP , and MySQL using the Agile method, and 33 respondents evaluated it using ISO/IEC 25010, giving an overall mean of 4.77 (Excellent). The authors reported that it improved reporting accuracy , reduced response time, and improved coordination. This closely matches the GPS reporting, status updates, and monitoring dashboard of GAOIRS (Consumo et al., 2026). Project SIGNAL (Strategic Incident Georeferencing for Nationwide Alertness) Project SIGNAL is an incident reporting and monitoring system of DOST Region 6 for local government units, used for emergencies such as landslides, floods, vehicular accidents, and medical emergencies. Incidents are reported 3 1 through a mobile application that automatically geo-tags the location and sends it to the Emergency Operations Center of the LGU, and the recorded data builds a database for planning and risk analysis. The system was implemented in 2022 and had recorded more than 27,000 incident reports as of April 2025, and 132 of the 139 LGUs in W estern V isayas had activated their accounts. DOST said it aims to expand the platform, including to the Negros Island Region. This is the closest local example of GAOIRS, which combines geotagged reporting with verification before dispatch, nearest responder dispatch, and trend forecasting for the NIR (Clarin, 2025). Bandilyo App: A Disaster Risk Reduction Monitoring and Incident Reporting System with Geolocation and SMS T echnology Bandilyo App is a disaster risk reduction and incident reporting system built for the Municipality of T anauan, Leyte, that combines geolocation and SMS technology in an integrated command-and-control communication system. The developers used Agile methodology to build a mobile application, a web application, and a client-server architecture that supports coordination and humanitarian response activities. The Municipal DRRMO reported that the system supported ef fective, accurate, and timely information for coordination. This is a close local example of the kind of geolocation-based incident reporting and coordination system GAOIRS is built to provide for DRRMO and BFP personnel in the Negros Island Region (de los Santos et al., 2020). 3 2 2.5 Synthesis T able 1 Synthesis of Related Literature R e l a t e d S t u d i e s F e a t u r e s a n d F u n c t i o n a l i t i e s D e v e l o p e d S y s t e m C o m p r e h e n s i v e A n a l y s i s o f t h e U s e o f W e b - e v a l u a t i v e a s s e s s m e n t o f L G U D R R M a c r o s s f o u r t h e m a t i c a r e a s • A l l a r e a s r a t e d e f f e c t i v e ; p r e p a r e d n e s s r a t e d h i g h e s t • S u p p o r t e d t h e n e e d f o r p r a c t i c a l t o o l s s u c h a s G A O I R S t o h e l p D R R M O / B F P c a r r y o u t N D R R M C - c a t e r e d d u t i e s w i t h a v a i l a b l e r e s o u r c e s A c c e p t a n c e o f M o b i l e A p p l i c a t i o n o n D i s a s t e r P r e p a r e d n e s s ( E s c o l a n o e t a l . , 2 0 2 3 ) • U s e r a c c e p t a n c e o f a d i s a s t e r - p r e p a r e d n e s s a p p s t u d i e d • A c c e p t a n c e f r a m e d a s k e y t o d e c i s i o n i n t e l l i g e n c e • S u p p o r t e d e v a l u a t i n g G A O I R S f o r u s a b i l i t y w i t h a c t u a l e n d u s e r s The reviewed literature shows that ef fective incident-response platforms combine location-aware reporting, GIS-based visualization, and dashboards that track cases from report to resolution. The foreign studies show that mapping incident, dispatch, and response records reveals where and when cases cluster , that citizen reports need reliability checks before use, and that GIS tools underperform when user access, knowledge, and implementation are neglected. The local studies reinforce this by showing that mapping incidents in space and time exposes high-risk areas and response gaps across dif ferent hazards and regions, whether for emergencies in Oton, Iloilo, fires in Pampanga and Manila, or road crashes in Quezon City , and that DRRM of fices need both a working tool and adequate personnel and funding to act on what the data shows. 3 6 In summary , GAOIRS draws on this literature by combining evidence-based citizen reporting, report verification, nearest-responder dispatch, GIS mapping and hotspot detection, and Prophet-based trend forecasting into a single platform built for the DRRMO and BFP . It aligns with the direction of the reviewed studies by turning separate findings on spatial analysis, dashboard design, and reliability filtering into one system developed specifically for NDRRMC-catered incidents in the Negros Island Region. a n d - c o n t r o l s y s t e m • M o b i l e a p p , w e b a p p , a n d c l i e n t - s e r v e r a r c h i t e c t u r e b u i l t w i t h A g i l e • M u n i c i p a l D R R M O r e p o r t e d e f f e c t i v e , a c c u r a t e , t i m e l y c o o r d i n a t i o n • C l o s e l o c a l e x a m p l e o f g e o l o c a t i o n - b a s e d i n c i d e n t r e p o r t i n g a n d c o o r d i n a t i o n f o r D R R M O / B F P p e r s o n n e l i n t h e N I R The reviewed systems show that citizen reports paired with location data can be collected and routed to responders through web and mobile platforms, as demonstrated by Ushahidi, Project SIGNAL, the GPS-based system in Lambunao, iReportMo, and iAlerto, while MOSAIC and the digital twin prototype 3 8 show that shared dashboards and automatic updates support coordination among multiple agencies. Most of these systems, however , address only one part of the incident-response process, such as reporting, mapping, alerting, or pattern analysis, rather than the process as a whole. In summary , GAOIRS addresses this gap by bringing evidence-based reporting, verification, nearest-responder dispatch, map and hotspot visualization, trend forecasting, and role-based dashboards into one platform. While the reviewed systems each cover part of what GAOIRS does, few combine all of these functions for the DRRMO and BFP together , which is the gap that GAOIRS is designed to fill. 3 9 CHAPTER III Methodology This chapter introduces the systematic approach and tools used to develop and evaluate the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration. The study adopts the Agile Development Method framework, which provides a structured process for planning, designing, building/developing, testing, deploying, reviewing and launching the system. The chapter outlines the operational framework that guided the project and presents key modeling tools including the Context Diagram and Data Flow Diagrams (DFD) to illustrate system processes, the Entity-Relationship Diagram (ERD) to define data structures, and the Unified Modeling Language (UML) Use Case Diagram and scenarios to describe user interactions. A complete Data Dictionary was provided to define all data elements used in the system. The chapter also specified the necessary hardware and software requirements, presented the project timeline, and discussed the evaluation process, which includes system testing and assessment using the ISO/IEC 25010:201 1 Software Quality Model and ISO/IEC 9241-1 1:2018 Software Quality Model to ensure functionality , reliability , and overall user experience. The chapter concluded with the identification of respondents involved in the system assessment and the ethical considerations observed throughout the study . 3.1 Systems Development Method Systems Development Method refers to the framework used to structure, plan, and control the information system development process. The study 4 0 employed a combination of developmental and descriptive research methods: developmental research focused on the design, development, testing, and refinement of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration (GAOIRS), while descriptive research was used to evaluate the system's functionality , usability , and performance through testing procedures and feedback gathered from DRRMO and BFP personnel. T o guide the system development process, the researchers adopted the Agile methodology , specifically an incremental development model, over a plan-driven model such as W aterfall. Agile was chosen because the requirements of the DRRMO and BFP Station became clearer step by step through interviews, feedback sessions, and prototype reviews, and each cycle produced a working part of the system that could be tested and shown to the users. This iterative emphasis on continuous feedback and testing throughout development helped ensure that the system's features matched the actual workflow of its intended users. 4 1 Figure 1 Agile Development Method Figure 1 shows the seven stages of each development cycle: Plan, Launch, Design, Develop, T est, Deploy , and Review . The Review stage feeds its results back into the next Plan stage, so the system improves with every cycle. Plan In the Plan stage, the researchers conducted interviews with DRRMO and BFP Station personnel and observed their existing workflows to identify the requirements, challenges, and priorities for each development cycle. Information was gathered on how incidents are currently reported, verified, dispatched, and recorded. These findings were used to define the system's core functions, including incident report recording and management, response dispatch, geospatial mapping, monitoring, and report generation, which became the backlog of features for the cycle. 4 2 Design In the Design stage, the researchers prepared and refined the interface mockups, data flow diagrams, and database design based on feedback from the previous review . Each feature scheduled for the cycle, such as the incident reporting form, the geospatial mapping interface, or the analytics dashboard, was planned in detail before development began. Develop In the Develop stage, the modules were coded and integrated in increments: role-based access control, incident report recording and management, incident response and dispatch, geospatial mapping and geofencing, real-time monitoring and heatmap visualization, trend analysis and analytics, and report generation. Partial features could be tested and shown to users before the whole system was assembled. T est In the T est stage, the modules were tested individually and as one integrated system. White-box testing checked the internal logic and back-end functions, and black-box testing checked the system from the user's side. Errors were fixed and the af fected parts were tested again before moving forward. Deploy In the Deploy stage, the tested version of the system was released to a hosted environment so that DRRMO and BFP personnel could try it under conditions similar to actual use. 4 3 Review In the Review stage, the team evaluated the deployed system by gathering feedback from the DRRMO and BFP personnel. The identified issues, user suggestions, and system performance results were documented and analyzed to determine the necessary improvements before the final release. Launch In the Launch stage, the researchers reviewed the backlog with DRRMO and BFP Station stakeholders to confirm the scope and priority of the features to be built in the current cycle. The timeline, task assignments, and resources needed to complete the cycle were also set during this stage before development work began. 4 4 3.2 Operational Framework Figure 2 Operational Framework of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration Figure 2 shows how the three system actors interact with the platform. The Citizen (reporter) submits incident details, including GPS location, photo, and incident type, and receives confirmation and status updates. The Response Unit (BFP or DRRMO) sends acknowledgement and status updates, and receives automated alerts with incident data and maps. The Administrator receives the emergency reports, configures role-based access control and system settings, and receives dashboard views, heatmaps, and generated reports. The data are stored in and retrieved from a central database. 4 5 3.3 Data Flow Diagram Figure 3 Context Diagram of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration Figure 3 shows the context diagram of the system, highlighting how Citizens, Response Units (CDRRMO and BFP), and Administrators interact with it. Citizens submit incident emergency reports, while Response Units send their login credentials, incident acknowledgments, incident status updates, and response documentation. Administrators send login credentials, incident verification decisions, dispatch commands, user account management data, and report generation requests. The system sends back report submission confirmations and report status updates to Citizens. It sends login authentication, incident dispatch notifications, and incident details and location to Response Units. For Administrators, it returns new and pending incident reports, interactive map data, incident response updates, analytics and heatmap data, audit log 4 6 data, and post-incident reports to help each user perform their duties properly and keep incident records organized. Figure 4 Level 1 Data Flow Diagram of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration Figure 4 illustrates the Level 1 Data Flow Diagram of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration. It expands the main process into five interconnected functions that show how data moves throughout the system. Citizens begin the workflow by submitting incident details such as the type, location, and photo to 1.0 Incident Reporting and Capturing. These details were stored in D1 Incident Records, and the Citizen received a report submission confirmation and a reference number . The verified incident details then passed to 2.0 Incident V erification and Management, where the Administrator (System In-charge) classified and verified the report and issued 4 7 the travel order for dispatch. This process saved incident type data in D6 Incident T ypes, and exchanged evidence and status updates with D7 Evidence and Incident Status Logs. The incident details were then forwarded to 3.0 Incident Response and Dispatch, which retrieved responder information and availability from D4 Response Units, recorded assignment and response data in D3 Incident Assignments, and stored notifications in D8 Notifications. Response Units (CDRRMO and BFP) received dispatch orders and incident updates, and sent back acknowledgments, response status, and documentation. The incident location and response data moved on to 4.0 Geospatial Monitoring and Analysis, which used map data, incident locations, and barangay and district boundaries, and stored and retrieved geospatial data in D5 Barangay and District Data. This process gave users the interactive map, heatmap, and analytics data. Finally , the incident data and analytics flowed to 5.0 Report Generation and System Administration. The Administrator (System Administrator) sent report generation requests, user management data, and system settings, and received report summaries, audit logs, and system updates. This process saved generated reports in D10 Generated Reports, post-incident reports in D1 1 Post-Incident Reports, and audit records in D9 System Audit Logs. 4 8 Figure 5 Level 2 Data Flow Diagram of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration Figure 5 presents the Level 2 Data Flow Diagram of the system, showing how citizens, administrators, and response units report, verify , dispatch, and monitor incidents. The process begins with the Citizen submitting incident details, which are captured, validated against the incident types, and classified before being stored as incident records. The Admin (System In-charge) then verifies and 4 9 reviews the report and confirms the dispatch, updating the incident status and creating the assignment based on the availability of response units. The Response Unit (CDRRMO and BFP) views and acknowledges the dispatch order , is assigned the unit and resources, and updates its deployment status. Response data is then encoded and analyzed to produce maps, heatmaps, and analytics data, and the results are used to generate reports and post-incident records. Finally , the Admin (System Administrator) manages users and roles, reviews system audit logs, and performs maintenance and backup. The diagram shows a clear flow of data to support accurate recording, proper verification, coordinated dispatch, and informed monitoring of incidents. 5 0 Figure 6 Level 3 Data Flow Diagram of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration Figure 6 presents the Level 3 Data Flow Diagram of the system, showing selected Level 2 processes broken down into three sub-processes each. Process 1.1 Capture Incident Details begins when the Citizen (Reporter) submits the incident type, location, photo, and description. The system inputs the information, validates the data format, and stores the incident record in D1 Incident Records, then returns an acknowledgment and reference number to the Citizen. In 2.1 V erify Incident Report, the Admin (System In-charge) provides the report classification, verification, and travel order . The report is reviewed, the information is validated, and the report status is updated in D1 Incident Records, 5 1 with a confirmation and travel order details sent back to the Admin. In 3.1 V iew and Acknowledge Dispatch Order , the Response Unit (CDRRMO and BFP) receives the dispatch order , acknowledges it, and confirms the assignment, which is saved in D3 Incident Assignments. In 4.1 Encode Response Data, the Response Unit enters the response data, which is validated and saved as a response record in D1 1 Post-Incident Reports. The Response Unit then receives maps, heatmaps, and analytics data. Finally , in 5.1 Manage Users and Roles, the Admin (System Administrator) sends the account management data and system settings. The system manages the user accounts, assigns user roles, and updates access permissions in D2 User Accounts/Roles, then returns system updates and account information to the Admin. 5 2 Figure 7 Level 4 Data Flow Diagram of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration Figure 7 presents the Level 4 Data Flow Diagram of the system, showing the most detailed steps inside five Level 3 processes. In 1.1.1 Input Incident Information, the Citizen (Reporter) enters the incident details, attaches an incident photo, and submits the information. The submitted report is saved in D1 Incident Records, and the Citizen receives an acknowledgment and reference number . In 2.1.1 Review Report, the Admin (System In-charge) sends the report details. The system retrieves the incident report, reviews the report details, and confirms the review , with the confirmation saved in D1 Incident Records and a confirmation or review result returned to the Admin. In 3.1.1 V iew Dispatch Order , the Response Unit (CDRRMO and BFP) sends the dispatch order , and the 5 3 system receives it and displays the assignment details. The Response Unit then acknowledges the dispatch, which is saved in D3 Incident Assignments, and receives an acknowledgment and response details in return. In 4.1.1 Enter Response Data, the Response Unit enters the response details, the system validates the response information, and the response record is submitted to D1 1 Post-Incident Reports as post-incident data. Lastly , in 5.1.1 Manage User Accounts, the Admin (System Administrator) sends the account data. The system creates or updates the user account, assigns the user role, and updates the access permissions in D2 User Accounts/Roles. T ogether , the diagrams show a clear flow of data to support accurate recording, proper verification, coordinated dispatch, and secure management of users. 5 4 3.4 Entity Relationship Diagram Figure 8 Entity Relationship Diagram of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration Figure 8 shows the Entity Relationship Diagram (ERD) of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration 5 5 (GAOIRS), which includes the main entities such as User , Incident, Response Unit, Incident Assignments, Barangays, Districts, Incident T ypes, Evidence, Incident Status Log, Notifications, Generated Reports, and Post-Incident Reports. The Incident table serves as the central entity that stores the details of reported incidents, including the incident type, location, description, status, severity , priority , and reporter information. The User table is connected to incidents, assignments, status logs, evidence, generated reports, and post-incident reports to identify the users responsible for submitting, managing, updating, or reviewing system records. The Response Unit table stores information about BFP and DRRMO units and is linked to incident assignments and notifications to support the dispatch and monitoring of response operations. The Incident Assignments table records the assignment of verified incidents to response units, including assignment status and response timestamps. The Barangays and Districts tables provide geographic reference information used to associate users, response units, and incidents with their corresponding jurisdictions. The Incident T ypes table stores the categories and related attributes of incidents used for classification and map visualization. The Evidence table stores photos and other files uploaded as supporting evidence for reported incidents, while the Incident Status Log records changes in incident status for monitoring and tracking purposes. The Notifications table stores messages sent to assigned response units regarding incident assignments and updates. The Generated Reports table records reports produced by authorized users, while the Post-Incident Reports table stores information about actions taken, response 5 6 time, casualties, damages, and other details recorded after an incident has been resolved. The ERD illustrates how these entities are related and how the database supports incident recording, verification, response dispatch, monitoring, documentation, and reporting within the GAOIRS. 5 7 3.5 UML Use Case Diagram Figure 9 Use Case Diagram of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration Figure 9 shows what each actor can do in the system. The Citizen can submit an incident report, select the incident type, tag the GPS location, upload photo evidence, track the report status, and receive a submission confirmation. The Response Unit can acknowledge incidents, update and resolve incident 5 8 status, view the interactive map, and upload response reports and evidence. The Administrator has the widest access: managing incidents, dispatching response teams, generating reports, viewing the analytics dashboard, managing user accounts, and configuring incident types and access roles. All actors sign in through the Authentication use case (Use Case 1). The diagram sets clear boundaries so that each function is available only to authorized roles. 3.6 Use Case Scenario T able 3 Submit Report U s e C a s e 1 : S u b m i t R e p o r t U s e C a s e G o a l : T o a l l o w a c i t i z e n t o r e p o r t a n i n c i d e n t a n d h a v e i t q u e u e d f o r a d m i n v e r i f i c a t i o n .
