M M - D D H H : M M : S S 1 9 D a t e a n d t i m e o f t h e a c t i o n 2 0 2 6 - 0 6 - 0 1 1 1 : 0 1 : 0 0 3.8 Hardware and Software Requirements Hardware and Software Requirements provide detailed minimum requirements needed to support the developed system's functions and executions Recommended Minimum Hardware Requirements ● Computer: desktop or laptop with internet access ● Processor: multi-core, at least 2.0 GHz ● RAM: at least 8 GB ● Storage: at least 100 GB of free space for system files, records, evidence, and reports ● Network: stable internet, at least 10 Mbps download speed 7 5 ● GPS-capable smartphone or tablet: required for capturing GPS coordinates when reporting from the field Recommended Software Requirements ● Operating System: Windows 1 1 or macOS ● Front end: React.js, V ite, and T ailwind CSS ● Back end: Node.js and Express.js, with RESTful APIs and W ebSocket for real-time updates ● Database: PostgreSQL ● Mapping and charts: Leaflet.js for maps, geofencing, and nearest responder detection; Chart.js for charts ● Analytics: Python (KDE) ● Hosting: Cloud hosting (V ercel for the web application) ● V ersion control: Git 3.9 Project T imeline The development of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration (GAOIRS) of ficially began on March 16, 2026, and is scheduled for completion on September 22, 2026, as presented in the project Gantt Chart. The project follows an Agile Development Life Cycle, covering the major stages of planning and analysis, system design, development, integration and testing, deployment and review , and documentation and closure. The timeline organizes the development activities into six major phases, with overlapping activities used where appropriate to support continuous system development and evaluation. The Planning and Analysis Phase ran from March 16 to April 10, 2026. During this period, the proponents established the data-gathering plan and 7 6 coordinated with relevant stakeholders. Interviews and workflow observations were conducted with the DRRMO and BFP Station to understand existing incident-response processes and identify operational requirements. The proponents then conducted process gap analysis, developed the feature backlog, and validated the project scope. The completion of this phase provided the requirements and scope needed to proceed with system design. From April 13 to May 1, 2026, the project proceeded to the System Design Phase. This phase focused on defining the overall structure and technical design of GAOIRS. The proponents prepared the module listing, context diagram, Data Flow Diagram (DFD), and Use Case Diagram to represent the system processes and user interactions. Database design was also carried out through the development of the Entity-Relationship Diagram (ERD) and Data Dictionary . In addition, interface mockups and prototype reviews were conducted to establish the intended user interface and system workflow before development began. The Development Phase was conducted from May 4 to July 24, 2026, covering seven development sprints. The phase began with environment setup and database creation, followed by the implementation of role-based access control and account management. Subsequent development focused on incident report recording and management, incident verification, response and dispatch, geospatial mapping and geofencing, real-time monitoring and heatmap visualization, and trend analysis and analytics with forecasting. The final development sprint focused on report generation and post-incident reports, completing the major functional components of GAOIRS. 7 7 The Integration and T esting Phase ran from July 27 to August 19, 2026. The developed modules were first integrated and the system environment was configured to ensure that the dif ferent components could operate together . White-box testing was then conducted to examine the back-end logic and functions, while black-box testing evaluated the system from the user side on a per-module basis. Identified defects were addressed through remediation and regression retesting to verify that corrections did not introduce new problems and that the integrated system continued to function as intended. The Deployment and Review Phase took place from August 17 to September 1 1, 2026. During this stage, GAOIRS was deployed to a hosted environment for evaluation. IT expert evaluation was conducted using the ISO/IEC 25010:201 1 software quality model, while end-user evaluation was conducted to assess the system from the perspective of its intended users. The results of these evaluations were subjected to statistical analysis, after which the proponents reviewed the feedback and performed the necessary final system revisions. The final stage, the Documentation and Closure Phase, began on September 1 and continued through September 22, 2026. This phase involved the preparation of the User ’ s Guide, followed by final manuscript revisions and the completion of the required appendices. The documentation activities were scheduled alongside the final stages of system evaluation and revision, allowing the project documentation to incorporate the finalized system and evaluation 7 8 results. The project timeline concludes with the completion of the final manuscript and appendices on September 22, 2026. Overall, the GAOIRS project timeline provides a structured progression from planning and requirements gathering, through system design and development, to testing, deployment, evaluation, and final documentation. The overlapping activities shown in the Gantt Chart support the Agile approach by allowing related development and review activities to progress within the same project period while maintaining defined deliverables for each phase. This timeline serves as the schedule for the systematic development, validation, and completion of the GAOIRS project. 3.10 System Evaluation T ools White-Box T esting For the White-Box T esting, Jest with Node.js was used to evaluate the internal logic and backend functionality of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration (GAOIRS). This testing approach focused on examining the behavior of individual functions, data processing, input validation, authentication, incident management, and database-related operations by executing unit tests that directly assessed the system's code behavior . Jest was used to test backend functions and verify whether they produced the expected results under valid, invalid, and boundary input conditions. The testing covered core processes such as user authentication, incident creation and management, report verification, incident 7 9 assignment, status updates, and other backend operations. Error-handling and validation conditions were also tested to identify logical errors and ensure that the system responded correctly to unexpected or invalid inputs. Overall, White-Box T esting allowed the researchers to examine the internal program logic, identify coding errors, and verify that the backend functions of GAOIRS operated according to the intended system requirements. Black-Box T esting For the Black-Box T esting, Playwright and Qase was used to evaluate GAOIRS from the user's perspective without examining the internal source code. Playwright was used to automate browser-based test scenarios and simulate actual user interactions with the system, while Qase was used to organize, document, and manage the test cases and their corresponding results. The testing covered major system functions such as user authentication, citizen incident reporting, report verification, incident assignment, geospatial mapping, incident monitoring, response status updates, report generation, and other role-specific functionalities. The test scenarios were designed to verify whether the system produced the expected results based on dif ferent user inputs and actions. Each test case recorded the expected behavior , actual behavior , and test result to determine whether the corresponding function passed or failed. Overall, Black-Box T esting allowed the researchers to assess the functionality , usability of system interactions, and correct behavior of GAOIRS under actual user scenarios. 8 0 System Quality and Usability Instruments Software quality was evaluated with a questionnaire based on the ISO/IEC 25010:201 1 model (International Organization for Standardization, 201 1), which has eight characteristics: functional suitability (whether the system has the needed functions and gives correct results), performance ef ficiency (response time and use of resources), compatibility (working with other systems and environments), usability (ease of learning and use), reliability (consistent performance), security (access control and data protection), maintainability (ease of modification), and portability (working on dif ferent devices and environments). Usability was evaluated with a questionnaire based on ISO 9241-1 1:2018 (ISO, 2018), which defines ef fectiveness (whether users can complete tasks accurately), ef ficiency (the ef fort and time needed), and satisfaction (comfort and acceptability of use). Both instruments used a five-point Likert scale. The findings from these evaluations were used to determine whether the system met user expectations and ef fectively supported the completion of tasks in an ef ficient manner . (See Appendix A for reference) T able 32 Likert Scale, Range, and V erbal Interpretation for the ISO/IEC 25010:201 1 and ISO 9241-1 1:2018 Evaluations R e s p o n s e S c a l e R a n g e V e r b a l I n t e r p r e t a t i o n S t r o n g l y A g r e e 5 4 . 2 0 – 5 . 0 0 V e r y H i g h A g r e e 4 3 . 4 0 – 4 . 1 9 H i g h U n d e c i d e d 3 2 . 6 0 – 3 . 3 9 A v e r a g e D i s a g r e e 2 1 . 8 0 – 2 . 5 9 L o w S t r o n g l y D i s a g r e e 1 1 . 0 0 – 1 . 7 9 V e r y L o w 8 1 The ISO/IEC 25010:201 1 Software Quality Model was used to evaluate the overall quality of the developed Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration (GAOIRS). The model evaluates software quality through characteristics including Functional Suitability , Performance Ef ficiency , Compatibility , Usability , Reliability , Security , Maintainability , and Portability . In this study , the respondents rated the system using a five-point Likert scale ranging from Strongly Disagree to Strongly Agree. The responses were assigned numerical values from 1 to 5, with higher scores indicating a more favorable assessment of the system. The computed mean scores were interpreted using the corresponding verbal interpretation shown in the table above. This evaluation was used to determine the extent to which GAOIRS met the defined software quality criteria. The ISO 9241-based usability evaluation was used to assess the usability of the developed GAOIRS from the perspective of its intended users. The evaluation focused on how ef fectively , ef ficiently , and satisfactorily users were able to interact with and accomplish tasks using the system. In this study , respondents rated the usability statements using a five-point Likert scale ranging from Strongly Disagree to Strongly Agree. Each response was assigned a numerical value from 1 to 5, and the resulting mean scores were interpreted according to the ranges and verbal interpretations presented in the table. The evaluation was used to determine the perceived usability of GAOIRS in terms of ease of use, understanding, interaction, and user satisfaction. 8 2 3.1 1 Respondents of the Study The respondents were chosen because of their direct involvement in incident response or their knowledge of software. Five IT experts evaluated the quality of the system using ISO/IEC 25010:201 1. T wenty-five end users, made up of four Response Unit personnel and twenty-one citizens, evaluated its usability using ISO 9241-1 1:2018. T able 33 Respondents of the Study R e s p o n d e n t F r e q u e n c y P e r c e n t I T E x p e r t s 5 1 6 . 7 % R e s p o n s e U n i t P e r s o n n e l 4 1 3 . 3 % C i t i z e n s 2 1 7 0 . 0 % T o t a l 3 0 1 0 0 . 0 % The study involved a carefully selected group of 30 respondents who participated in the evaluation of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration (GAOIRS). The respondents included IT experts who assessed the system's technical quality and functionality , Response Unit personnel composed of two BFP personnel and two DRRMO personnel who evaluated the system's incident monitoring, verification, dispatch, and response-related functions, and citizen respondents who assessed the incident reporting and status-tracking features from the perspective of the intended reporting users. The respondents were selected based on their relevant involvement with information systems, disaster incident response, or citizen 8 3 incident reporting, allowing the researchers to obtain feedback from both technical evaluators and actual or intended system users. 3.12 Ethical Consideration The researchers prepared formal request letters and sought permission from the appropriate of fices and intended participants before conducting interviews, workflow observations, system testing, and evaluation activities for the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration (GAOIRS). The purpose, scope, objectives, and procedures of the study were clearly explained to the participating personnel and respondents, particularly those involved in the Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Of fice (DRRMO) and Bureau of Fire Protection (BFP). Participation in the study was conducted voluntarily , and respondents were given suf ficient information about their involvement before data collection and system evaluation activities were conducted. The researchers strictly maintained the confidentiality and privacy of information obtained throughout the study . Since GAOIRS involves incident reports that may contain location information, descriptions, photographs, and other potentially sensitive details, collected information was handled carefully and was accessed only by authorized persons involved in the study . Personal information and respondent-related information obtained during interviews, observations, and system evaluations were not disclosed to unauthorized individuals and were used only for purposes directly related to the academic 8 4 research and development of GAOIRS. Appropriate measures were observed to protect collected data from unauthorized access, alteration, disclosure, or misuse. Access to system information and evaluation results was limited according to the researchers' authorized roles. The researchers also ensured that information presented in the study , including incident-related data, system evaluation responses, and other collected materials, was handled in a manner that protects the identity and privacy of the individuals involved. In compliance with the Data Privacy Act of 2012 (Republic Act No. 10173), informed consent was obtained from participants before collecting information from them. Participants were informed about the purpose of data collection, the manner in which their information would be used, and the measures implemented to protect their information. The researchers respected the participants' right to participate voluntarily and ensured that information collected during the study was used only for legitimate academic and system-development purposes. The researchers also maintained responsible and accurate handling of incident information throughout the development and evaluation of GAOIRS. Incident data were treated as information requiring verification before being used for response-related functions. This is consistent with the system's design, where citizen-submitted incident reports undergo verification before being dispatched to an appropriate response unit. Finally , the researchers observed ethical standards throughout the planning, development, testing, deployment, and evaluation of GAOIRS. Regular checks were undertaken to ensure that the collection, processing, storage, and 8 5 presentation of information remained consistent with the agreed research purpose, confidentiality requirements, and applicable data-protection principles. These measures were implemented to protect the rights and privacy of participants and to promote responsible research conduct throughout the study . 8 6 CHAPTER IV RESUL TS AND DISCUSSION This chapter presents the results of the study based on the objectives of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration (GAOIRS). It includes the developed system’ s modules and interfaces, the results of system testing, and the evaluation results conducted using the ISO/IEC 25010:201 1 Software Quality Model and ISO 9241-1 1:2018 usability measures. The chapter presents the dif ferent functionalities of GAOIRS, including incident report recording and management, incident verification and response dispatch, geospatial mapping and geofencing, real-time monitoring and heatmap visualization, trend analysis and forecasting, report generation, and role-based access control. The system evaluation involved five (5) IT experts who assessed the technical quality and functionality of GAOIRS based on the eight quality characteristics of ISO/IEC 25010:201 1. T wenty-five (25) end users, consisting of four (4) Response Unit personnel and twenty-one (21) citizens, evaluated the system based on ef fectiveness, ef ficiency , and satisfaction using ISO 9241-1 1:2018. Responses were gathered using a five-point Likert scale, ranging from 1 (Strongly Disagree) to 5 (Strongly Agree). This chapter also discusses the findings obtained from the system testing and evaluation. The results provide an overview of the system’ s functionality , technical quality , usability , and user experience. Furthermore, the discussion interprets the results in relation to the objectives of the study , identifies the system’ s observed strengths and areas requiring improvement, and presents the implications of the findings for the use and further enhancement of GAOIRS. 8 7 4.1 Results of the Study The first objective was to design and develop GAOIRS with five features: incident report recording and management, incident response, a geospatial mapping interface, monitoring and analysis, and security and report generation. The result was a working system with all five features. It records and manages incident reports, coordinates and tracks the response, shows incidents on an interactive map, provides trend analysis and a seven-day forecast, and enforces role-based security with report generation. The following figures show each feature. Figure 10 Incident Report Recording Interface Figure 10 shows the Incident Report Recording interface, the entry point for reports. The form has fields for photo evidence (up to five photos), the incident location with automatic GPS detection and a map pin that can be 8 8 adjusted, the emergency type, the severity (Low , High, or Critical), and the reporter ’ s name and contact number . The description is filled in from the selected type and severity . A warning that false reporting is punishable by law is shown near the Submit button. After submission, the report gets a unique incident code and is saved to the central database for verification, mapping, and monitoring. Figure 1 1 Incident Management Interface 8 9 Figure 1 1 shows the Incident Queue, where administrators review , verify , update, and track reports. The page has a search bar , status tabs (Responding, On Scene, Resolved, and False Alarm), and a severity filter . Each report card shows the incident code, type, severity , and status, and the selected report opens a panel with the incident details, a brief description, and a map of the precise location. An export button lets users download the records. Figure 12 Incident Response Interface 9 0 Figure 12 shows the Incident Response module. The upper screen is the Response Units page, where administrators see the registered units, their type, availability , base location, and contact details, and can register , edit, or remove a unit. The lower screen is the Live Response Map used by response units. It shows the units and the active incidents, counts of responding and on-scene incidents, and a pop-up with the incident details and options to get directions, request backup, and confirm arrival. T ogether , they support assigning responders and tracking the response until the incident is resolved. 9 1 Figure 13 Geospatial Mapping Interface Figure 13 shows the Geospatial Mapping interface on the administrator dashboard. Summary cards show the active incidents, registered users, resolved incidents, and total incidents. The map plots incidents with markers colored by severity and can switch between a marker view and a heatmap view . Selecting a marker shows the incident details, the reporter information, and the evidence photo, with buttons to approve and dispatch or reject the report. The side list groups incidents by new , responding, and reported. The figures on the cards come from the test and demonstration data used in the study . 9 2 Figure 14 Monitoring and Analysis Dashboard Figure 14 shows the Monitoring and Analysis module. The upper screen shows key indicators (total incidents, average response time, active incidents, and resolved incidents), the distribution of incidents by type, the ranking of barangays by number of incidents, and the seven-day forecast produced by the Prophet model with the average predicted incidents, the trend, and the peak and low days. The lower screen shows the kernel density estimation (KDE) heatmap of incidents, with intensity from low to critical. These help decision-makers see 9 3 patterns and plan resources. The values shown come from the synthetic demonstration data described in Section 3.8. Figure 15 Login and Role-Based Access Interface Figure 15 shows the login page of the Security module. Registered users sign in with an email and password, and the system opens the dashboard for their role. Unregistered citizens can use the link for reporting an emergency without logging in. Role-based access control keeps administrator and response unit functions available only to authorized users, which protects the confidentiality and integrity of the incident data. 9 4 Figure 16 Report Generation and Export Interface Figure 16 shows the Incident Archive page, which supports report generation. It shows the total archived, resolved, closed, and false alarm incidents, a searchable table of archived incidents, and an Export menu with Excel, CSV , and PDF options. Authorized users can use it to produce records for documentation, monitoring, and administrative work. The second objective was to test the functionality of the features. The system went through alpha testing by the researchers and IT experts, using black-box 9 5 and white-box tests, and beta testing by end users, namely Response Unit personnel and citizens, who were able to sign in, submit and track reports, and view mapped incidents. The following tables show the alpha test results. In the tables, Pass means the actual outcome matched the expected outcome, Fail means it did not, and Retest means the outcome could not be fully confirmed. Black-box testing checked each module from the user ’ s side, without looking at the code. T C - V e r i f R p t - 0 0 5 V e r i f i c a t i o n p e r m i s s i o n r e s t r i c t e d t o A d m i n r o l e O n l y u s e r s w i t h A d m i n c r e d e n t i a l s s h o u l d b e N o n - a d m i n a c c o u n t s w e r e c o r r e c t l y d e n i e d a c c e s s P a s s A c c e s s c o n t r o l w o r k e d a s i n t e n d e d . 9 9 a b l e t o v e r i f y o r r e j e c t i n c i d e n t r e p o r t s . t o t h e v e r i f i c a t i o n f u n c t i o n . The results in T able 34 demonstrate the functionality of the Incident Report Recording and Management module of GAOIRS. The test cases covered the visibility and operation of the incident reporting form, incident type selection, automatic location detection, photo attachment, report submission, confirmation and reference number generation, required field validation, and manual map pinning when GPS detection is unavailable. The testing also covered incident tracking, incident list display , filtering, searching, report verification, and related incident management functions. The results show that the major user-facing functions for recording, submitting, tracking, and managing incident reports were tested according to their expected behavior . P a s s I n t e g r a t i o n w i t h d i s p a t c h w o r k e d a s i n t e n d e d . The results in T able 35 demonstrate the functionality of the Incident Response module of GAOIRS. The test cases covered the display of incident notifications, acknowledgment of assigned incidents, notification of administrators, prevention of duplicate acknowledgments, response status updates, real-time status reflection, status-change notifications, and restrictions on updating incidents before acknowledgment. These results show that the module supports the major response activities from receiving an assigned incident through updating, documenting, and closing the response. These tests were conducted to verify that authentication information was properly processed and that unauthorized requests were appropriately restricted. The results show that the tested security and utility functions performed according to their expected behavior . T able 54 ISO/IEC 25010:201 1 Software Quality Evaluation Results (n = 5 IT Experts) Criteria Mean Score V erbal Interpretation Functional Suitability 4.23 V ery High Performance Ef ficiency 4.10 High Compatibility 4.25 V ery High Usability 4.53 V ery High 1 1 9 Reliability 4.35 V ery High Security 4.42 V ery High Maintainability 4.20 V ery High Portability 4.67 V ery High Overall A verage Mean 4.43 V ery High T able 54 shows that the system obtained an overall average mean of 4.34, interpreted as V ery High, based on the evaluation of five (5) IT experts using the ISO/IEC 25010:201 1 Software Quality Model. Among the eight evaluated characteristics, Portability received the highest mean score of 4.67, while Performance Ef ficiency received the lowest mean score of 4.10, interpreted as High. The remaining seven characteristics were interpreted as V ery High. Overall, the results indicate that the IT experts gave a V ery High assessment of the software quality of GAOIRS. The third objective also included evaluating usability using ISO 9241-1 1:2018, which covers ef fectiveness, ef ficiency , and satisfaction. The evaluation was answered by twenty-five (25) end users, made up of four (4) Response Unit personnel and twenty-one (21) citizens. T able 55 ISO 9241-1 1:2018 Usability Evaluation Results (n = 25 End Users) Measure Mean Score V erbal Interpretation Ef fectiveness 4.42 V ery High Ef ficiency 4.54 V ery High Satisfaction 4.58 V ery High 1 2 0 Overall A verage Mean 4.51 V ery High T able 55 shows that the system obtained an overall average mean of 4.51, interpreted as V ery High, based on the evaluation of twenty-five (25) end users consisting of four (4) Response Unit personnel and twenty-one (21) citizens using ISO 9241-1 1:2018. Among the three evaluated measures, Satisfaction received the highest mean score of 4.58, followed by Ef ficiency with 4.54, while Ef fectiveness received the lowest mean score of 4.42. All three measures were interpreted as V ery High. Overall, the results indicate that the end users gave a V ery High assessment of the usability of GAOIRS in terms of ef fectiveness, ef ficiency , and satisfaction. T aken together , the two evaluations show that GAOIRS was well received by IT experts and end users. They also point to performance ef ficiency and response time as areas for further improvement. The fourth objective was to develop a User ’ s Guide for the system. The guide was developed and explains how the system works for each user role: Response Unit personnel are guided in recording and responding to incidents, and Administrators in managing user accounts, monitoring system data, and generating reports. The complete guide is in the appendices. 1 2 1 Figure 17 User ’ s Guide Cover Page Figure 17 shows the cover page of the User ’ s Guide for GAOIRS. The guide serves as a reference for the intended users in understanding and navigating the system’ s features and functionalities, including incident reporting, incident response, system monitoring, and report generation. 1 2 2 CHAPTER V CONCLUSION AND RECOMMENDA TION This chapter presents the conclusions drawn from the development, testing, and evaluation of the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration (GAOIRS). It consolidates the key results of the study and discusses how the developed system addressed the major challenges in incident reporting and response, particularly delayed reporting, uncoordinated response, and limited access to timely spatial and analytical information for NDRRMC-catered incidents in the Negros Island Region. The findings highlight the system’ s contribution to improving incident reporting and management, coordinating response activities, providing geospatial visualization, and supporting monitoring and data-driven analysis. Furthermore, this chapter includes practical recommendations based on the study’ s outcomes to sustain and enhance the system’ s implementation. 5.1 Summary of Findings The study's overall results showed that all objectives were successfully achieved. The researchers integrated the technical features outlined in the first objective into the system: incident report recording and management, incident response, geospatial mapping with identification of the city and congressional district of incidents, monitoring and analysis, and report generation. They tested its functionality through alpha testing using both Black-box and White-box methods, with all 78 black-box and all 40 white-box test cases passing, and through beta testing by Response Unit personnel and citizens, thereby fulfilling 1 2 3 the second objective. Regarding the third objective, the system's quality was measured through an ISO standard questionnaire assessing the eight characteristics of ISO/IEC 25010:201 1. IT experts gave an average score of 4.34, interpreted as 'V ery High,' indicating high quality from the experts' perspective. Additionally , following ISO 9241-1 1:2018, end users gave mean scores of 4.42 in ef fectiveness, 4.54 in ef ficiency , and 4.58 in satisfaction, with an overall mean of 4.51, all interpreted as 'V ery High.' T o help users learn to operate the system ef fectively , the researchers also developed a User's Guide that clearly explains GAOIRS's capabilities and how it should be properly used by Response Unit personnel and Administrators, thereby fulfilling the fourth objective. 5.2 Conclusions This study aimed to address the critical challenges faced by the City Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Of fice (CDRRMO) and the Bureau of Fire Protection (BFP) in handling NDRRMC-catered incidents in the Negros Island Region. These included delays in incident reporting, uncoordinated response among responding units, fragmented records kept through phone calls, text messages, and two-way radios, and limited access to timely spatial and analytical information. T o solve these issues, the Geospatial Approach to Optimize Incident Response System with Data Analytics Integration (GAOIRS), developed a system using the Agile methodology , successfully fulfilled all objectives. The study's findings indicate that GAOIRS was fully implemented with all required features, passed all white-box and black-box test cases, received 1 2 4 V ery High evaluations from IT experts and end users, and was supported by a comprehensive User's Guide. The first objective was successfully achieved when GAOIRS was designed and fully developed with all five required features. The system integrated incident report recording and management with photo evidence, automatic GPS detection, and unique incident codes. It also included incident response with response unit registration, assignment, and live status tracking. A geospatial mapping interface identified the city and congressional district of incidents through geofencing and switched between marker and heatmap views. Monitoring and analysis provided incident indicators, barangay rankings, a kernel density estimation heatmap, and a seven-day Prophet forecast. Report generation allowed records to be exported as Excel, and PDF , and role-based access control secured the whole system. These features allowed the DRRMO and BFP to move from fragmented, logbook-based processes to a centralized digital system. The analytics were generated from synthetic data, so they demonstrated that the features worked but did not yet reflect real incident patterns. The second objective was fully achieved. During the alpha and beta phases, white-box and black-box testing were carried out, which resulted in a 100% pass rate across all test cases. All 40 white-box test cases passed, covering authentication, report generation, access control, incident management, and backend response handling. All 78 black-box test cases also passed across incident reporting, incident response, geospatial mapping, monitoring and 1 2 5 analysis, and report generation. Beta testing showed that Response Unit personnel and citizens could sign in, submit and track reports, and view mapped incidents. This confirmed the system's reliability and its readiness for deployment. The third objective was successfully achieved, yielding V ery High results. An evaluation conducted by five IT experts using ISO/IEC 25010:201 1 resulted in an overall mean score of 4.34, categorized as V ery High. Among the criteria, Portability scored the highest at 4.67, while Performance Ef ficiency scored the lowest at 4.10, interpreted as High. Similarly , twenty-five end users, composed of four Response Unit personnel and twenty-one citizens, assessed the system using ISO 9241-1 1:2018. They produced mean scores of 4.42 for Ef fectiveness, 4.54 for Ef ficiency , and 4.58 for Satisfaction, with an overall mean of 4.51, all interpreted as V ery High. These findings demonstrated that GAOIRS was highly functional, ef ficient, and provided a satisfying user experience. The fourth objective was successfully achieved with the development of a comprehensive User's Guide. The guide explained how the system worked for each user role, guiding Response Unit personnel in recording and responding to incidents and Administrators in managing user accounts, monitoring system data, and generating reports. This empowered users to operate the system confidently and fully utilize its features. 1 2 6 5.3 Recommendations The City Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Of fice (CDRRMO) and the Bureau of Fire Protection (BFP) were strongly encouraged to adopt GAOIRS as their of ficial platform for incident reporting and response. They were also encouraged to hold regular training sessions for Response Unit personnel, administrators, and new users, especially on the mapping and analytics features. This approach helped ensure confident and ef ficient use of the system during emergencies. The DRRMO and BFP were further advised to encode their actual incident records into the system, so that the heatmaps and trend analysis could reflect real incident patterns in each congressional district. The developers were advised to optimize the system's performance and response time as incident and geospatial records grow , since Performance Ef ficiency was the lowest-rated criterion in the IT experts' evaluation. Local government units and policymakers were urged to support similar digital initiatives by providing funding and by adopting policies that standardize incident reporting tools across the region. For future research and development, improvements such as stronger of fline reporting, expanded forecasting with more historical data, and integration with other local government and emergency response platforms were recommended. Additionally , conducting a longitudinal study to evaluate the system's long-term ef fect on response times, with more Response Unit respondents and tests in other localities of the Negros Island Region, could of fer valuable evidence for wider adoption. In conclusion, GAOIRS showed how a centralized, geospatial approach can turn fragmented reporting into faster , better-coordinated response, 1 2 7 and how purposeful, user-centered technology can strengthen the communities it serves. 1 2 8 REFERENCES Abdalla, R.
